376208 of A. Volovich.

Appendix A
Review of classical elliptic functions

In this appendix, we list our conventions for the classical elliptic functions.

Theta functions are periodic functions that can be represented by series whose convergence is extraordinarily rapid.
They provide an important means for numeric evaluation of elliptic functions.
Moreover, theta functions are useful for the solving algebraic equations involving elliptic functions.
For theta functions, we use the conventions of
rademacher2012topics
.
We list, for
q=\exp(i\pi\tau)
and
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
, all the Jacobi theta functions

\displaystyle\theta_{1}\!\left(\zeta,\tau\right)=2{e}^{\pi i\tau/4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\left(-1\right)}^{n}{q}^{n\left(n+1\right)}\sin\!\left(\left(2n+1\right)\pi\zeta\right),

(292)

\displaystyle\theta_{2}\!\left(\zeta,\tau\right)=2{e}^{\pi i\tau/4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q}^{n\left(n+1\right)}\cos\!\left(\left(2n+1\right)\pi\zeta\right),

(293)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}\!\left(\zeta,\tau\right)=1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{q}^{{n}^{2}}\cos\!\left(2n\pi\zeta\right),

(294)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}\!\left(\zeta,\tau\right)=1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\left(-1\right)}^{n}{q}^{{n}^{2}}\cos\!\left(2n\pi\zeta\right).

(295)

The factor
q^{n^{2}}
usually leads to rapid convergence of these series.

Equally important are Weierstraß functions.
The so-called Weierstraß
\wp
-function is doubly periodic and defined as

\begin{split}\wp\!\left(z,\tau\right)&=\frac{1}{{z}^{2}}+\sum_{\left(m,n\right)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus\left\{\left(0,0\right)\right\}}\frac{1}{{\left(z+m+n\tau\right)}^{2}}-\frac{1}{{\left(m+n\tau\right)}^{2}}\\
&={\left(\pi\theta_{2}\!\left(0,\tau\right)\theta_{3}\!\left(0,\tau\right)\frac{\theta_{4}\!\left(z,\tau\right)}{\theta_{1}\!\left(z,\tau\right)}\right)}^{2}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}}{3}\left(\theta_{2}^{4}\!\left(0,\tau\right)+\theta_{3}^{4}\!\left(0,\tau\right)\right),\end{split}

(296)

for
z\in\mathds{C},\tau\in\mathbb{H}
.
Then, the Weierstraß
\zeta
-function is a primitive for the
\wp
-function

\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}z}\,\zeta\!\left(z,\tau\right)=-\wp\!\left(z,\tau\right)\iff\zeta(z)=z^{-1}-\int_{0}^{z}\textnormal{d}w(\wp(w)-w^{-2}).

(297)

This gives a convenient way to express the integral of the
\wp
-function

\int_{0}^{z}\textnormal{d}w\wp(w)=-\zeta(z)+z^{-1}+\int_{0}^{z}\textnormal{d}ww^{-2}.

(298)

With a bit of work, one can work out the following relation to the Jacobi theta functions

\zeta\!\left(z,\tau\right)=-\frac{z}{3}\frac{\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\!\left(0,\tau\right)}{\theta^{\prime}_{1}\!\left(0,\tau\right)}+\frac{\theta^{\prime}_{1}\!\left(z,\tau\right)}{\theta_{1}\!\left(z,\tau\right)}.

(299)

Moreover, the series representation is given by

\zeta\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\frac{1}{z}+\sum_{\left(m,n\right)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\setminus\left\{\left(0,0\right)\right\}}\frac{1}{z-m-n\tau}+\frac{1}{m+n\tau}+\frac{z}{{\left(m+n\tau\right)}^{2}}.

(300)

The
\wp
- and
\zeta
-functions have the following useful analytic properties:

1.

\mathop{\operatorname{poles}\,}\limits_{z\in\mathbb{C}}\wp\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\Lambda_{(1,\tau)}
(double pole at zero),

2.

\mathop{\operatorname{poles}\,}\limits_{z\in\mathbb{C}}\zeta\!\left(z,\tau\right)=\Lambda_{(1,\tau)}
(only simple poles),

3.

\mathop{\operatorname{zeros}\,}\limits_{z\in\mathbb{C}}\wp\!\left(z,i\right)=\left\{\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\right)+\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)i:m\in\mathbb{Z}\;\mathbin{\operatorname{and}}\;n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
,

4.

\wp\!\left(z,\tau\right)\text{ is holomorphic on }z\in\mathbb{C}-\Lambda_{(1,\tau)}
,

5.

\zeta\!\left(z,\tau\right)\text{ is holomorphic on }z\in\mathbb{C}-\Lambda_{(1,\tau)}
.

Clearly,
\zeta(z)
is parity-odd – i.e.,
\zeta(-z)=-\zeta(z)
.

Appendix B
Properties of generic quartic elliptic curve

In this appendix, we review some basic facts about elliptic curves.
Much of the material covered here is borrowed from
Broedel:2017kkb
;
Broedel:2018iwv
;
farkas1992riemann
;
mckean_moll_1997
. Conventions in this appendix are independent to those in the body of this paper.

To start, consider a family
E(\mathbb{C})
of quartic elliptic curves defined by the irreducible polynomial equation with unspecified roots
e_{i}

E(\mathbb{C}):y^{2}-(x-e_{1})(x-e_{2})(x-e_{3})(x-e_{4})=0.

(301)

Assuming the canonical ordering
-\infty<e_{1}<e_{2}<e_{3}<e_{4}<\infty
and choosing the positive branch, we set

\sqrt{y^{2}}=+\sqrt{|y^{2}|}\times\begin{cases}1&x\leq e_{1},\\
i&e_{1}<x\leq e_{2},\\
1&e_{2}<x\leq e_{3},\\
i&e_{3}<x\leq e_{4},\\
1&e_{4}<x.\end{cases}

(302)

With this convention, the periods of the (framed)
elliptic curve
can be written as

\psi_{1}=2c_{4}\int_{e_{2}}^{e_{3}}\frac{\textnormal{d}x}{y}=2K(k)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\psi_{2}=2c_{4}\int_{e_{2}}^{e_{1}}\frac{\textnormal{d}x}{y}=2iK(1-k),

(303)

where
K
stands for the complete elliptic integrals of
1^{\textnormal{st}}
kind and

k:=\frac{e_{14}e_{23}}{e_{13}e_{24}}\in(0,1)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad c_{4}:=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{e_{13}e_{24}}>0,

(304)

such that
\tau:=\psi_{2}/\psi_{1}\in\mathbb{H}
. In particular, given
k\in(0,1)
, we have
\tau\in i\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Working with
\{1,\tau\}
instead of
\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}
gets rid of the
\textnormal{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})
normalization ambiguity.

For completeness, we may also introduce the quasi-periods of the differential form

\textnormal{d}x\hat{\Phi}_{4}(x,y):=-\frac{\psi_{1}^{2}\textnormal{d}x}{4c_{4}^{2}y}\left(x^{2}-\frac{s_{1}}{2}x+\frac{s_{2}}{6}\right),

(305)

which has zero residue by design and where
s_{n}:=s_{n}(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
and (later used)
\bar{s}_{n}:=s_{n}(e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
are two elementary symmetric polynomials in four and three variables respectively. They are given by combinations of complete elliptic integrals of
1^{\textnormal{st}}
and
2^{\textnormal{nd}}
kinds

\phi_{1}^{\prime}:=\frac{c_{4}}{\psi_{1}}\int_{e_{2}}^{e_{3}}\textnormal{d}x\hat{\Phi}_{4}(x,y)=\psi_{1}\times\left(E(k)-\frac{1}{3}(2-k)K(k)\right),

(306)

and

\phi_{2}^{\prime}:=\frac{c_{4}}{\psi_{1}}\int_{e_{2}}^{e_{1}}\textnormal{d}x\hat{\Phi}_{4}(x,y)=\psi_{1}\times\left(\frac{1}{3}i(k+1)K(1-k)-iE(1-k)\right).

(307)

The periods and quasi-periods are not independent, but they are related through the
Legendre’s relation

\phi_{2}^{\prime}-\tau\phi_{1}^{\prime}=-i\pi.

(308)

The periods and quasi-periods are strictly speaking not invariants of
E(\mathbb{C})
, since there may be different values of
\{1,\tau\}
and
\{\phi_{1}^{\prime},\phi_{2}^{\prime}\}
that correspond to the same elliptic curve.
32
32

32

For example, we can perform a global rescaling of the periods without changing the geometry.

There is however an invariant, called the
j
-invariant
, that uniquely characterizes an elliptic curve. For quartic elliptic curve, it is given by

j=256\frac{(1-k(1-k))^{3}}{k^{2}(1-k)^{2}}.

(309)

This invariant diverges where the elliptic discriminant
\Delta
vanishes. Mathematically, the elliptic curve becomes singular (nodal) since at least two roots of the quartic need to collide. Physically, this happens as a Landau surface is approached in the kinematic space.

The map from the elliptic curve to its
torus
is provided by the
Abel’s map

E(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}/\Lambda_{(1,\tau)}:\quad(x,y)\to Z\equiv\frac{c_{4}}{\psi_{1}}\int_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x=\lambda(Z)}}\frac{\textnormal{d}x^{\prime}}{y}\quad\textnormal{mod}\ \Lambda_{(1,\tau)},

(310)

where the formal inverse
\lambda
will be defined soon. Since the lattice
\Lambda_{(1,\tau)}
is invariant under the action of the
modular group

\textnormal{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, any two framed elliptic curves of the form (
301
) with respective periods
\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}
and
\{\psi_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{2}^{\prime}\}
are equivalent provided

\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{2}^{\prime}\\
\psi_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\gamma\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{2}\\
\psi_{1}\end{pmatrix},

(311)

for a
(2\times 2)
integral matrix in
\gamma\in\textnormal{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

Conversely, the map from the torus to the elliptic curve is given by

\mathbb{C}/\Lambda_{(1,\tau)}\to E(\mathbb{C}):\quad Z\to[\lambda(Z):c_{4}\psi_{1}^{-1}\lambda^{\prime}(Z):1].

(312)

where
\lambda
denotes the formal inverse of (
310
) and is given by

\lambda(Z):=\frac{-3e_{1}e_{13}e_{24}\psi_{1}^{-2}~\wp(Z)+e_{1}^{2}\bar{s}_{1}-2e_{1}\bar{s}_{2}+3\bar{s_{3}}}{-3e_{13}e_{24}\psi_{1}^{-2}~\wp(Z)+3e_{1}^{2}-2e_{1}\bar{s}_{1}+\bar{s}_{2}},

(313)

From its dependence in the
Weierstraß
\wp
-function
,
\lambda
is a doubly periodic and meromorphic function. Therefore,
\lambda
is an
elliptic function
. Furthermore
\lambda(\pm Z_{a})=a
. Solving for
\wp
in (
313
), we find

\small\wp(Z)=\frac{{-}(e_{3}e_{4}{+}e_{2}(e_{3}{+}e_{4}))(2e_{1}{+}\lambda(Z)){+}e_{1}((e_{2}{+}e_{3}{+}e_{4})(e_{1}{+}2\lambda(Z)){-}3e_{1}\lambda(Z)){+}3e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}}{3\psi_{1}^{{-}2}~(e_{1}{-}e_{3})(e_{2}{-}e_{4})(e_{1}{-}\lambda(Z))}.

(314)

Since we are working with a
quartic
elliptic curve, let’s clarify what we mean by
\wp(Z)
. To do this, we need to find the Weierstraß form of the quartic elliptic curve in (
301
).

The canonical way to proceed is as follows: Starting with (
301
), we send a finite root, say
e_{1}
, at (complex) infinity using the rational map

x\to\frac{1}{x-e_{1}}\implies e_{1}\to e^{\prime}_{1}=\infty,e_{2\leq i\leq 4}\to e^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{1}{e_{i}-e_{1}}.

(315)

Then, we translate the coordinate
x
to put a root at zero using

x\to x-e_{2}^{\prime}\implies e_{1}^{\prime}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\infty,e_{2}^{\prime}\to e_{2}^{\prime\prime}=0,e_{3\leq i\leq 4}\to e_{i}^{\prime\prime}=e_{i}^{\prime}-e_{2}^{\prime}.

(316)

Finally, we scale by

\displaystyle x\to x\frac{(e_{1}-e_{2})(e_{1}-e_{4})}{\psi_{1}^{-2}~(e_{2}-e_{4})},

(317)

\displaystyle\implies e^{\prime\prime}_{1}\to e_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\infty,e_{2}^{\prime\prime}\to e_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}=0,e_{3}^{\prime\prime}\to e_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\psi_{1}^{2}k,e_{4}^{\prime\prime}=\psi_{1}^{2}.

(318)

The effect of these changes of variables on
y
is

y^{2}\to Y^{2}=x(x-\psi_{1}^{2})(x-\psi_{1}^{2}k).

(319)

This is a Legendre-like normal form. To get the Weierstraß normal form we seek
R_{1},R_{2}
and
R_{3}
such that

\psi_{1}^{2}k=R_{3}-R_{2},\ \psi_{1}^{2}=R_{1}-R_{2},\ R_{2}-R_{2}=0\ :R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3}=0\iff R_{2}:=-\frac{1+k}{3\psi_{1}^{-2}}.

(320)

In terms of the original roots,

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2e_{2}e_{3}+e_{1}(e_{2}+e_{3}-2e_{4})+(e_{2}+e_{3})e_{4}}{3\psi_{1}^{-2}~(e_{1}-e_{3})(e_{2}-e_{4})},

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2e_{3}e_{4}+e_{2}(e_{3}+e_{4})+e_{1}(-2e_{2}+e_{3}+e_{4})}{3\psi_{1}^{-2}~(e_{1}-e_{3})(e_{2}-e_{4})},

(321)

\displaystyle R_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{2}(e_{3}-2e_{4})+e_{3}e_{4}+e_{1}(e_{2}-2e_{3}+e_{4})}{3\psi_{1}^{-2}~(e_{1}-e_{3})(e_{2}-e_{4})}.

Then,
\wp
is the solution to the following differential equation

(\wp^{\prime}(Z))^{2}=4(\wp(Z)-R_{1})(\wp(Z)-R_{2})(\wp(Z)-R_{3}).

(322)

From this as well as (
314
) it is not hard to check that

\begin{split}(c_{4}\psi_{1}^{-1}\lambda^{\prime}(Z))^{2}=\prod_{i=1}^{4}(\lambda(Z)-e_{i}),\end{split}

(323)

thus justifying (
310
).

Note that, by the negation property of elliptic curves, if
Z_{a}=(a,y(a))
then
-Z_{a}=(a,-y(a))
. From this set-up, we can easily deduce that

\lambda(0)=e_{1},\ \lambda(\pm\tau/2)=e_{2},\ \lambda(\pm(1+\tau)/2)=e_{3},\ \ \lambda(\pm 1/2)=e_{4}.

(324)

Moreover,
\lambda(Z)
has poles for
Z_{\infty}
such that
\lambda(\pm Z_{\infty})=\infty
. These are given by (
310
)

\pm Z_{\infty}:=\frac{c_{4}}{\psi_{1}}\int_{\infty}\frac{\textnormal{d}x^{\prime}}{y}.

(325)

Appendix C
The isomorphism between the elliptic curve and the torus

In this section, we detail the change of variable from
(X_{0},X_{2},X_{3})
to
(\tau,z_{1},z_{2})

Bogner:2019lfa
.

Recall, that in section
4.2
we defined the distance

\displaystyle z_{x_{1}}=Z_{x_{1}}-(-Z_{x_{1}})=2Z_{x_{1}}\equiv\frac{F(\sin^{-1}(u_{x_{1}})|k)}{K(k)}\quad\textnormal{mod}\ \Lambda_{(1,\tau)},

(326)

where
k=\frac{r_{23}r_{14}}{r_{13}r_{24}}\in(0,1)
and

u_{x_{1}}=\sqrt{\frac{(x_{1}-r_{1})(r_{2}-r_{4})}{(x_{1}-r_{2})(r_{1}-r_{4})}}.

(327)

In terms of the dimensionless variables kinematic variables, it is easy to check that

k=-\frac{16X_{0}X_{2}X_{3}}{(++-)(+-+)(-++)(---)},

(328)

and we define

u_{1,\infty}^{2}:=\lim_{x_{1}\to\infty}u_{x_{1}}^{2}|_{m_{1}\leftrightarrow m_{2}}=-\frac{(++-)(---)}{4X_{3}},

(329)

as well as

u_{2,\infty}^{2}:=\lim_{x_{1}\to\infty}u_{x_{1}}^{2}|_{m_{1}\leftrightarrow m_{3}}=-\frac{(+-+)(---)}{4X_{2}}.

(330)

Since the point
u_{c}
is tied to the particularities of the loop-by-loop fibration, we prefer use
u_{2,\infty}
instead.
We are now ready to give the change of variable
\{X_{0},X_{2},X_{3}\}\leftrightarrow\{\tau,z_{1},z_{2}\}
. On one hand we have

\{\tau,z_{1},z_{2}\}=\left\{\frac{\psi_{2}}{\psi_{1}},\frac{F(\sin^{-1}(u_{1,\infty})|k)}{K(k)},\frac{F(\sin^{-1}(u_{2,\infty})|k)}{K(k)}\right\},

(331)

while on the other the introduction of an intermediate set of variables is needed. To motivate this, we need to refer to the Jacobian elliptic sn-function defined as the formal inverse of
F=F(\sin^{-1}(x)|k)
– i.e.,

F=\int_{0}^{x=\textnormal{sn}(F|k)}\frac{\textnormal{d}x}{\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-k^{2}x^{2})}}.

(332)

In view of (
326
), this definition implies

K(k)\ z=\int_{0}^{x=\textnormal{sn}(K(k)z|k)}\frac{\textnormal{d}x}{\sqrt{(1-x^{2})(1-k^{2}x^{2})}}.

(333)

The connection to Jacobi’s
\theta
-functions comes from the identities (given
q=\exp(i\pi\tau)
)

\displaystyle\sqrt{k}=\frac{\theta_{2}^{2}(0|q)}{\theta_{3}^{2}(0|q)}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\textnormal{sn}(F|k)=\frac{1}{k^{1/4}}\frac{\theta_{1}\left(\frac{\pi F}{2K(k)}|q\right)}{\theta_{4}\left(\frac{\pi F}{2K(k)}|q\right)}

(334)

\displaystyle\implies\textnormal{sn}(K(k)z|k)=\frac{\theta_{3}(0|q)}{\theta_{2}(0|q)}\frac{\theta_{1}\left(\pi\frac{z}{2}|q\right)}{\theta_{4}\left(\pi\frac{z}{2}|q\right)}.

(335)

In particular, these give raise to the following intermediate variables

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}:=k

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta_{2}^{4}(0|q)}{\theta_{3}^{4}(0|q)},

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}:=u_{1,\infty}^{2}=\frac{\theta_{3}^{2}(0|q)}{\theta_{2}^{2}(0|q)}\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}\left(\pi\frac{z_{1}}{2}|q\right)}{\theta_{4}^{2}\left(\pi\frac{z_{1}}{2}|q\right)},

\displaystyle\quad\kappa_{2}:=u_{2,\infty}^{2}=\frac{\theta_{3}^{2}(0|q)}{\theta_{2}^{2}(0|q)}\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}\left(\pi\frac{z_{2}}{2}|q\right)}{\theta_{4}^{2}\left(\pi\frac{z_{2}}{2}|q\right)}.

(336)

Solving for
\{X_{0},X_{2},X_{3}\}
in the above, one finds (specific to our root ordering)

X_{0}^{2}=\frac{(\kappa_{1}-1)(\kappa_{2}-1)\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}}{(\kappa_{1}\lambda_{0}-1)(\kappa_{2}\lambda_{0}-1)},

X_{2}^{2}=\frac{(\kappa_{1}-1)\kappa_{1}}{(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2})^{2}(\kappa_{1}\lambda_{0}-1)(\kappa_{2}\kappa_{1}\lambda_{0}-\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}+1)^{2}}R,

(337)

X_{3}^{2}=\frac{(\kappa_{2}-1)\kappa_{2}}{(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2})^{2}(\kappa_{2}\lambda_{0}-1)(\kappa_{2}\kappa_{1}\lambda_{0}-\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{2}+1)^{2}}R,

where

\begin{split}R&=(\kappa_{1}{+}\kappa_{2})^{3}\lambda_{0}(\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\lambda_{0}{+}1){-}8\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}(\kappa_{1}{+}\kappa_{2})^{2}\lambda_{0}{+}8\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}(\kappa_{1}{+}\kappa_{2})(\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\lambda_{0}{+}1)\\
&{+}3\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}(\kappa_{1}{+}\kappa_{2})\lambda_{0}(\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\lambda_{0}{+}1){+}(\kappa_{1}{+}\kappa_{2})\left(\kappa_{1}^{3}\kappa_{2}^{3}\lambda_{0}^{3}{+}1\right){+}4\kappa_{1}^{2}\kappa_{2}^{2}(1{-}\lambda_{0})\lambda_{0}\\
&{-}8\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\left(\kappa_{1}^{2}\kappa_{2}^{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}{+}1\right){-}\left(\kappa_{1}^{2}{+}\kappa_{2}^{2}\right)(\lambda_{0}{+}1)\left(\kappa_{1}^{2}\kappa_{2}^{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}{+}1\right){-}8\kappa_{1}^{2}\kappa_{2}^{2}\\
&{+}2\left(\kappa_{1}^{2}\lambda_{0}{-}2\kappa_{1}{+}1\right)\sqrt{(\kappa_{1}{-}1)\kappa_{1}(\kappa_{2}{-}1)\kappa_{2}(\kappa_{1}\lambda_{0}{-}1)(\kappa_{2}\lambda_{0}{-}1)}\left(\kappa_{2}^{2}\lambda_{0}{-}2\kappa_{2}{+}1\right).\end{split}

(338)

Using, for example, sum of squares identities we can expand the kinematic variables in terms of the torus variables

X_{0}^{2}=\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{1}}{2}|q)\theta_{1}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{2}}{2}|q)\theta_{2}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{1}}{2}|q)\theta_{2}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{2}}{2}|q)}{\theta_{3}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{1}}{2}|q)\theta_{3}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{2}}{2}|q)\theta_{4}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{1}}{2}|q)\theta_{4}^{2}(\pi\frac{z_{2}}{2}|q)}.

(339)

Appendix D
Differential forms on the torus and relevant modular transformations

Kronnecker forms

Weinzierl:2020fyx
;
Weinzierl:2020kyq
are closed, quasi-periodic and meromorphic differential forms on the punctured torus. They are explicitly given by

\omega_{k}^{\textnormal{Kr}}(L(z),K\tau):=(2\pi i)^{2{-}k}\left(g^{(k{-}1)}(L(z),K\tau)\textnormal{d}L(z){+}K(k{-}1)g^{(k)}(L(z),K\tau)\frac{\textnormal{d}\tau}{2\pi i}\right),

(340)

where
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
L(z)
is a linear function

L(z):=\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{m}z_{m}+\beta.

(341)

The functions
g^{(\bullet)}
are the Laurent coefficients of the so-called
Eisenstein-Kronnecker function

F(x,y,\tau):=\frac{\pi\theta_{1}^{\prime}\left(e^{i\pi\tau}\right)\theta_{1}\left(\pi(x+y),e^{i\pi\tau}\right)}{\theta_{1}\left(\pi x,e^{i\pi\tau}\right)\theta_{1}\left(\pi y,e^{i\pi\tau}\right)}=\sum_{k\geq 0}g^{(k)}(x,y)y^{k-1},

(342)

A natural question is: “
Why Kronnecker forms
”? The answer to this question lies in the fact that the generating function
F
is the
unique
quasi-periodic meromorphic function of
\tau

and

z_{i}
on the torus
(
zagier1991periods
, Thm. 3)
. It follows that
\omega_{k,n}^{\textnormal{Kr}}
is the unique closed and quasi-periodic form in
\textnormal{d}\tau

and

\textnormal{d}z_{i}
with
simple poles
.

Since
g
-functions and Kronnecker forms have been studied extensively in the past few years and we refer the reader to
weil1999elliptic
;
Weinzierl:2020fyx
;
Weinzierl:2020kyq
;
Broedel:2017kkb
;
brown2013multiple
;
zagier1991periods
for more details. In particular, the modular transformation rule for
\omega_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{Kr}}
is
Weinzierl:2020fyx

\omega_{k}^{\textnormal{Kr}}\left(L^{\prime}\left(z^{\prime}\right),\tau^{\prime}\right)=\left(c\tau+d\right)^{k-2}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{k}\frac{1}{j!}\left(\frac{cL\left(z\right)}{c\tau+d}\right)^{j}\omega_{k-j}^{\textnormal{Kr}}\left(L\left(z\right),\tau\right).

(343)

The other important class of differential forms on the torus are
modular forms
. These are differential forms in
\tau
exclusively. In particular, the modular forms showing up in (
5.4.3
) are given by

\eta_{2}(\tau)=[e_{2}(\tau)-2e_{2}(2\tau)]\frac{\textnormal{d}\tau}{2\pi i},

(344)

and

\eta_{4}(\tau)=e_{4}(\tau)\frac{\textnormal{d}\tau}{(2\pi i)^{3}},

(345)

where the
e_{\bullet}
’s are
Eisenstein series
. In terms of standard elliptic functions, the relevant Eisenstein series are given by

e_{2}(\tau)=2\zeta\left(\frac{1}{2},\tau\right)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad e_{4}(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\theta_{2}^{8}\!\left(0,\tau\right)+\theta_{3}^{8}\!\left(0,\tau\right)+\theta_{4}^{8}\!\left(0,\tau\right)\right).

(346)

It is not demanding to show that
e_{2}(\tau)-2e_{2}(2\tau)\in\mathcal{M}_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(2))
and
e_{4}(\tau)\in\mathcal{M}_{4}(\textnormal{SL}(2,\mathds{Z}))
, where
\mathcal{M}_{k}(\Gamma)
denotes the set of modular forms of weight
k
in the subgroup
\Gamma
of
\textnormal{SL}(2,\mathds{Z})
. For more on modular form, we refer the reader to the nice review in
Adams:2017ejb
.

An understanding of the modular properties of the special functions appearing in the base basis was a crucial piece of knowledge in section
5
. In particular, we took all the algebraic functions of the kinematic to be modular invariant. Below we describe the modular transformation rules of the elliptic functions relevant to our derivation of (
5.4.3
).

First, it is easy to show that under a
\textnormal{SL}(2,\mathds{Z})
transformation we have (up to a
\textnormal{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})
-ambiguity)

\begin{cases}\psi_{2}\to(a\tau+b)\psi_{1},\\
\psi_{1}\to(c\tau+d)\psi_{1}.\end{cases}

(347)

Among other useful modular transformation rules are those for the partial derivatives of the standard kinematic variables with respect to the (punctured) torus parameters – e.g.,

\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial\tau},\quad\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{1}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{2}}.

(348)

These are found by applying the inverse function theorem. For instance, under the action of the modular group

\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial\tau}=\frac{\partial X_{i}(\tau,z_{1},z_{2})}{\partial\tau}\to\frac{\partial X_{i}(\tau^{\prime},z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})}{\partial\tau^{\prime}},

(349)

where

\tau^{\prime}=\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad z_{i}^{\prime}=\frac{z_{i}}{c\tau+d},

(350)

the inverse function theorem tells us that

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{1}^{\prime}}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}&=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial z_{1}}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial z_{2}}{\partial\tau^{\prime}}\\
\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial z_{1}^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial z_{1}}{\partial z_{1}^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial z_{2}}{\partial z_{1}^{\prime}}\\
\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial z_{2}^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial z_{1}}{\partial z_{2}^{\prime}}&\frac{\partial z_{2}}{\partial z_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial\tau}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{1}}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{2}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\tau^{\prime}}{\partial\tau}&\frac{\partial z_{1}^{\prime}}{\partial\tau}&\frac{\partial z_{2}^{\prime}}{\partial\tau}\\
\frac{\partial\tau^{\prime}}{\partial z_{1}}&\frac{\partial z_{1}^{\prime}}{\partial z_{1}}&\frac{\partial z_{2}^{\prime}}{\partial z_{1}}\\
\frac{\partial\tau^{\prime}}{\partial z_{2}}&\frac{\partial z_{1}^{\prime}}{\partial z_{2}}&\frac{\partial z_{2}^{\prime}}{\partial z_{2}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial\tau}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{1}}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}(c\tau+d)^{2}&c(c\tau+d)z_{1}&c(c\tau+d)z_{2}\\
0&c\tau+d&0\\
0&0&c\tau+d\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial\tau}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{1}}\\
\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial z_{2}}\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(351)

Appendix E
Details on the gauge transformations

In this appendix, we enumerate explicitly the expressions for matrix components of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{U}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}
defined, respectively, in (
102
) and (
114
). The former are given by

\small u_{75}=\frac{\psi_{1}}{\pi}\frac{\left(X_{0}^{2}+(X_{2}-X_{3})^{2}-1\right)(-++)(+--)(---)(+++)}{\mathcal{D}},

(352)

and

\small u_{76}=\frac{\psi_{1}}{\pi}\frac{\sqrt{\left(X_{0}^{2}{-}1\right)^{2}\left(X_{2}^{2}{-}X_{3}^{2}\right)^{2}}}{\left(X_{0}^{2}{-}1\right)}\frac{\left(5X_{0}^{4}{-}2\left(3X_{2}^{2}{+}3X_{3}^{2}{-}5\right)X_{0}^{2}{+}X_{3}^{4}{+}\left(X_{2}^{2}{-}1\right)^{2}{-}2\left(X_{2}^{2}{+}1\right)X_{3}^{2}\right)}{\mathcal{D}},

(353)

where the common denominator is given by

\mathcal{D}:=2~c_{4}~X_{0}^{2}~\left(-3X_{0}^{4}+2\left(X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}+1\right)X_{0}^{2}+X_{3}^{4}+\left(X_{2}^{2}-1\right)^{2}-2\left(X_{2}^{2}+1\right)X_{3}^{2}\right).

(354)

The latter are given by

\begin{split}v_{54}&=\frac{3N}{2i~\psi_{1}~\mathcal{D}}\left[\frac{\left(X_{0}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}-X_{3}^{2}-1\right)}{X_{3}}\frac{\partial X_{3}}{\partial\tau}+\frac{\left(X_{0}^{2}-X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}-1\right)}{X_{2}}\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial\tau}\right],\end{split}

(355)

\small\begin{split}v_{64}&=\frac{S~N}{2i~\psi_{1}~\mathcal{D}}\left[\frac{\left(3X_{0}^{2}-3X_{2}^{2}-X_{3}^{2}+1\right)}{X_{2}}\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial\tau}-\frac{\left(3X_{0}^{2}-X_{2}^{2}-3X_{3}^{2}+1\right)}{X_{3}}\frac{\partial X_{3}}{\partial\tau}\right],\end{split}

(356)

\begin{split}v_{75}&=\frac{iN}{\psi_{1}~\mathcal{D}}\left[\frac{\left(X_{0}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}-X_{3}^{2}-1\right)}{X_{3}}\frac{\partial X_{3}}{\partial\tau}+\frac{\left(X_{0}^{2}-X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}-1\right)}{X_{2}}\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial\tau}\right],\end{split}

(357)

\begin{split}v_{76}&=\frac{S~N}{i\psi_{1}~\mathcal{D}}\left[\frac{~\left(-3X_{0}^{2}+3X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}-1\right)}{X_{2}}\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial\tau}-\frac{\left(-3X_{0}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}+3X_{3}^{2}-1\right)}{X_{3}}\frac{\partial X_{3}}{\partial\tau}\right],\end{split}

(358)

and

\displaystyle v_{74}^{(\textnormal{I})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\psi_{1}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}\mathcal{D}^{2}}\bigg[9X_{0}^{12}{-}22X_{0}^{10}\left(X_{2}^{2}{+}X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right){-}X_{0}^{8}\left(X_{2}^{4}{-}50\left(X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)X_{2}^{2}{+}X_{3}^{4}{-}50X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad{+}4X_{0}^{6}\bigg(11X_{2}^{6}{-}19\left(X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)X_{2}^{4}{+}\left({-}19X_{3}^{4}{+}54X_{3}^{2}{-}19\right)X_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{+}\left(X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)\left(11X_{3}^{4}{-}30X_{3}^{2}{+}11\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad{-}X_{0}^{4}\bigg(41X_{2}^{8}{-}84\left(X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)X_{2}^{6}{+}\left(86X_{3}^{4}{+}52X_{3}^{2}{+}86\right)X_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{+}\left({-}84X_{3}^{6}{+}52X_{3}^{4}{+}52X_{3}^{2}{-}84\right)X_{2}^{2}{+}\left(X_{3}^{2}{-}1\right)^{2}\left(41X_{3}^{4}{-}2X_{3}^{2}{+}41\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad{+}2X_{0}^{2}(X_{2}{-}X_{3}{-}1)(X_{2}{-}X_{3}{+}1)(X_{2}{+}X_{3}{-}1)(X_{2}{+}X_{3}{+}1)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(5X_{2}^{6}{-}5\left(X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)X_{2}^{4}{+}\left({-}5X_{3}^{4}{+}2X_{3}^{2}{-}5\right)X_{2}^{2}{+}5\left(X_{3}^{2}{-}1\right)^{2}\left(X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad{+}\left(X_{2}^{4}{-}2\left(X_{3}^{2}{+}1\right)X_{2}^{2}{+}\left(X_{3}^{2}{-}1\right)^{2}\right)^{3}\bigg],

(359)

where the common numerator is given by

N=(-++)(++-)(+-+)(---),

(360)

while the sign function is given by

S=\textnormal{sign}\left(\frac{\left(X_{0}^{2}-1\right)\left(X_{2}^{2}-X_{3}^{2}\right)}{X_{0}^{4}}\right).

(361)

Additionally, it can be checked that

v_{75}=-\frac{2}{3}v_{54}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad v_{76}=-2v_{64}.

(362)

Finally, since
v_{74}^{\textnormal{(II)}}
is determined by the other
v
’s, we may write

\begin{split}\hskip-113.81102ptv_{74}^{\textnormal{(II)}}&=\frac{N^{2}\left(3\left(X_{0}^{2}-X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}-1\right)^{2}+\left(-3X_{0}^{2}+3X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}-1\right)^{2}\right)}{4\psi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}^{2}X_{2}^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial\tau}\right)^{2}\\
&+\frac{N^{2}\left(3\left(X_{0}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}-X_{3}^{2}-1\right)^{2}+\left(-3X_{0}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}+3X_{3}^{2}-1\right)^{2}\right)}{4\psi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}^{2}X_{3}^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial X_{3}}{\partial\tau}\right)^{2}\\
&+\frac{N^{2}~F_{74}}{2\psi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}^{2}X_{2}X_{3}}\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial\tau}\frac{\partial X_{3}}{\partial\tau},\end{split}

(363)

where

\begin{split}F_{74}&=\left(-6X_{0}^{2}-\left(3X_{0}^{2}-X_{2}^{2}-3X_{3}^{2}+1\right)\left(3X_{0}^{2}-3X_{2}^{2}-X_{3}^{2}+1\right)\right.\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\left.+3\left(X_{0}^{4}-\left(X_{2}^{2}-X_{3}^{2}\right)^{2}\right)+3\right).\end{split}

(364)
